Captain, that only affects my sleep... Besides, the Tarot Gathering is on Monday afternoon. Do I need to send Justice and The Hanged Man a notice postponing it? Klein shook his head with a wry smile, then said goodbye and left.

As he emerged from the stairwell, he sensed something and looked toward the carriage they had hired. Melissa was watching him silently through the window.

The instant their eyes met, she turned away and sat up straight.

The corner of Klein's mouth twitched. He climbed into the carriage and pretended nothing had happened.

Beneath the crimson moon and clear night sky, the carriage sped through one street after another.

When they arrived home, Klein let Benson use the bathroom first. He went to Melissa's bedroom and knocked twice.

Melissa, who had been about to use the other bathroom, opened the door and gave her brother a suspicious look.

"Melissa, is there something you want to ask me? I know there is," Klein said directly.

Please don't just watch me in silence...

Melissa pursed her lips and knitted her brows.

"Klein, what did you do to Elizabeth? She didn't seem like herself."

"And afterward, Selena began acting very strangely too."

Klein had his answer ready.

"You know Elizabeth and Selena are enthusiasts of mysticism, don't you?"

"Yes, but I don't approve. I don't believe there's anything in this world that can't be explained," Melissa replied earnestly after a moment's surprise. "Anything that seems inexplicable only appears that way because our knowledge is insufficient."

"Yes, I think so too," Klein agreed guiltily.

I used to think so as well, until I successfully courted death...

He coughed lightly and continued, "Mysticism involves Hermes, a language used specifically in ancient rituals of worship and prayer. Elizabeth knew I was familiar with it. After all, it's within a historian's field of study. She asked me how certain words were pronounced and what they meant."

Melissa nodded slightly, accepting the explanation. It was consistent with what she knew of both Klein and Elizabeth.

"As for why Elizabeth and Selena acted so strangely afterward, I don't know the actual reason," Klein said, first distancing himself from the matter. "But I can make a guess."

"You can?" Melissa blurted in surprise.

Klein raised a hand to his lips.

"I can infer it from what Elizabeth asked me. Several of the Hermes words concerned divination and the worship of evil entities. When Selena performed the magic-mirror divination, did she recite anything in Hermes?"

He had brought up the subject deliberately, hoping to put Melissa on guard against similar situations. Better still, she might distance herself from Selena and Elizabeth.

"Yes..." Melissa answered after a pause. "I think I understand why they were behaving so strangely."

Klein continued as though testing a theory. "Since Selena's magic-mirror divination involved a wicked and illicit faith, perhaps Elizabeth took the opportunity to criticize her and correct her mistake after confirming the meaning of the Hermes with me?"

"I think so." Melissa did not question the conclusion because she had arrived at the same deduction herself.

Seeing that he had successfully guided the conversation, Klein quietly breathed a sigh of relief.

"In the future, you should advise Selena to keep her faith with the orthodox churches."

He tapped four points on his chest in the manner of a priest.

"Yes, I will!" Melissa replied with conviction.

"And don't tell Elizabeth or Selena about our deduction or anything I've said. I promised Elizabeth I wouldn't tell you," Klein emphasized.

"All right." Melissa nodded.

At eight o'clock on Monday morning, Klein entered the Blackthorn Security Company.

He removed his hat and greeted Rozanne and Bredt. After exchanging a few words with them, he entered Captain Dunn Smith's office.

The moment he opened the door and looked inside, he was startled. Dunn's face was unusually pale, and his gray eyes looked cloudy, bereft of their customary depth.

"What happened? Was it Hanass Vincent?" Klein asked with alarm and concern.

Dunn rubbed his forehead, took a sip of coffee, and gave a bitter smile.

"Hanass Vincent is dead."

"Who got to him first?" Klein sat opposite Dunn, his cane still in hand.

Dunn did not answer immediately. He sighed.

"Leonard and I went to see Hanass Vincent last night. Nothing about his usual behavior appeared abnormal, and we found nothing unusual in his home, so I decided to enter his dream in search of clues."

"In his dream... In his dream..."

Fear surfaced in Dunn's eyes. After repeating himself twice, he finally continued.

"I saw a cross. An enormous cross that blotted out the sky. A naked man had been nailed to it with black spikes through his hands and feet. His arms were spread wide, but he hung upside down, his head dangling like a chandelier. Streaks of blood covered his body."

"The moment I saw it, I lost consciousness and was thrown out of Hanass Vincent's dream. When I awoke, Leonard told me Hanass had died in his sleep."

"A gigantic cross, a man hanging upside down, his body covered in blood... It resembles certain accounts of the True Creator believed by hidden organizations, but there are significant differences..." Klein reasoned aloud, puzzled.

The hidden organizations that worshiped the True Creator, such as the Aurora Order and the Iron and Blood Cross Order, had only appeared within the past two or three centuries. Yet similar imagery had persisted for well over a thousand years.

Dunn rubbed his forehead again. "We'll continue the investigation. For now, go complete your initiation mission."

Chapter 89: A Simple Mission

Klein nodded. "All right, but I still don't know what my mission is."

"It's nothing dangerous. At least, I haven't seen any indication of danger," Dunn emphasized. "The Golden Indus police station referred the case to us. For the past month, the renowned philanthropist Sir Deweyville has been subjected to some unusual harassment. Neither his bodyguards, the security personnel he hired, nor the police have been able to identify the culprit. Inspector Tolle, who is in charge of the case, suspects Beyonder powers may be involved, so he turned it over to us."

I saw Sir Deweyville at the library the other day and noticed how listless and exhausted he looked. So it was because of this harassment... Klein frowned. "What kind of harassment?"

Since no physical harm had been done, the situation apparently wasn't considered dangerous.

"Sir Deweyville hears moans and cries every night, no matter where he is, whether in Tingen or elsewhere. It has severely disrupted his sleep." Dunn leafed through the notes in his hands. "He consulted a psychiatrist and asked his butler and servants to verify what he heard. Once they confirmed it wasn't a hallucination, he concluded that someone was harassing him."

Dunn closed the file and looked up at Klein.

"Change into your probationary inspector's uniform in the break room, then meet Inspector Tolle at the Shooting Club. He'll give you the remaining details."

"A probationary inspector's uniform?" Klein asked instinctively.

Dunn rubbed his forehead and smiled.

"Half our salaries come from the police department, and 'probationary inspector' isn't merely a title on paper. Leonard and I were also in uniform when you first met us. This is one of the benefits afforded to official members. Yes, a 'perk,' as Emperor Roselle would call it."

Unfortunately, I can't wear it as ordinary clothing. Otherwise, I'd have a spare outfit whenever my clothes were being washed... Klein picked up his cane, bade Dunn farewell, and left the office.

In the break room, he found a black-and-white checkered uniform and a pair of leather boots laid out on the table. The peaked cap bore the police emblem: two crossed swords beneath a crown. Each black-and-white epaulet displayed a gleaming silver star.

"So this is a probationary inspector's uniform?" Klein examined it and noticed a series of numbers beneath the silver stars: 06-254.

He had a basic understanding of the Loen Kingdom's police hierarchy. At the top were the minister and chief secretary of police, followed by the commissioners, deputy commissioners, and assistant commissioners of the various departments. Superintendents and inspectors occupied the middle ranks, with sergeants and constables at the bottom.

After closing the door, Klein removed his suit and hat and changed into the uniform.

He hung up his suit, left the room, and entered the clerks' office. Rozanne brought over a full-length mirror so he could examine himself.

The young man in the reflection had black hair and gentle brown eyes. The uniform lent him a spirited, heroic air.

"Not bad." Klein shamelessly praised himself.

He left his cane in the office and departed the Blackthorn Security Company. His pockets held a full complement of equipment, from his weapons to his police badge.

At the Shooting Club's reception hall, Klein immediately spotted Inspector Tolle, who was the only other person in a police uniform.

Of course, I'm wearing one too... Klein thought.

Two silver stars adorned Tolle's epaulets. His belly strained against his uniform, and a thick blond mustache covered his upper lip. He was tall without being imposing, though perhaps he had possessed a more formidable figure in his younger days.

"Moretti? Klein Moretti?" Inspector Tolle greeted him with a smile.

"Good morning, Inspector Tolle. I believe you've found the right man." Klein returned the friendly smile. Drawing on his memories, he raised his right arm, held his fingers straight and together, and saluted.

Tolle chuckled.

"I can already tell you're an easy young man to work with. Good. Shall we head to Sir Deweyville's residence?"

Although he outranked Klein, his manner was notably cordial.

"Certainly." Klein considered for a moment. "You can brief me on the case during the ride."

"Of course." Tolle stroked his thick blond mustache and led Klein out of the Shooting Club. They boarded a carriage waiting across the road.

The police emblem of crossed swords and a crown decorated its side, and it came with a dedicated driver.

"Sir Deweyville is a believer in the Goddess, which is why we referred the case to your team," Tolle explained once they were seated.

"I know. The good knight often appears on the covers of newspapers and magazines." Klein smiled pleasantly.

Tolle picked up the document pouch beside him, broke the seal, and removed the papers inside. He flipped through them as he spoke.

"Even if you're already familiar with him, I must give you a thorough briefing.

"Sir Deweyville is one of Tingen's wealthiest magnates. He began his career with factories producing lead and porcelain, then expanded into steel, coal, shipping, banking, and bonds. He is also a prominent philanthropist who has received praise from the king. He established the Deweyville Charity Foundation, the Deweyville Trust, and the Deweyville Library, among other institutions. He was knighted five years ago. If he chose to run for mayor, I doubt anyone in Tingen could challenge him.

"But his true ambition lies in Backlund. He wants to become a member of Parliament. We once suspected the harassment might be connected to his political aspirations, but so far we've found no evidence."

Klein nodded slightly. "We can't eliminate the possibility, but at present there's nothing to support it."

Tolle did not linger on the point.

"Beginning on the sixth of last month, Sir Deweyville started hearing agonized, hair-raising moans every night as he slept, like a patient struggling through his final moments. He searched the surrounding rooms repeatedly but found nothing unusual. His butler and servants confirmed that they heard the sounds too, although they were much quieter for them.

"At first, Sir Deweyville assumed it would soon pass and paid little attention to it. But the moans grew increasingly frequent, sometimes occurring even during the day. Before long, they were accompanied by heartrending cries.

"Sir Deweyville repeatedly lost sleep and eventually fled Tingen for his country villa, but it did no good. The moaning and crying followed him. They persisted even in Backlund, though they weren't as severe there.

"He hired security guards to search his surroundings, but they found no clues. Our preliminary investigation was equally fruitless.

"After enduring this torment for more than a month, Sir Deweyville is close to collapse. He has consulted psychiatrist after psychiatrist, yet none could solve the problem. He told us that unless the matter is resolved within a month, he will leave Tingen for Backlund. He believes someone there will be able to help him."

As Tolle finished, Klein swiftly considered several possibilities.

Did he offend a Beyonder and become the victim of a curse?

No. If he were cursed, the butler and servants wouldn't hear the same sounds...

Could a Beyonder with unknown motives be hiding among his servants or bodyguards?

But no one has made any demands of Sir Deweyville in the past month...

Perhaps he accidentally encountered a vengeful evil spirit?

That possibility can't be ruled out...

While Klein pondered, the carriage entered the Golden Indus Borough and stopped outside Sir Deweyville's residence.

A steel fence enclosed a luxuriant garden. Two statues flanked the openwork metal gates, and a magnificent fountain sprayed water over a marble sculpture. Beyond it stood a broad two-story mansion, approached by a drive wide enough for three carriages abreast.

"Even a knight's residence is only two stories tall... Yet the newspapers say Backlund is experimenting with ten-story apartment buildings..." Klein stepped down from the carriage as a sergeant with three chevrons hurried toward them.

The sergeant looked at Klein and saluted.

"Good morning, sir!"

"Good morning." Klein smiled and nodded.

Tolle smiled as well. "This is Sergeant Gate. Let him know if you need anything.

"And this is Probationary Inspector Moretti, an expert in history and psychology from the police department," Tolle added, introducing Klein to Gate.

...I hardly deserve those titles. Klein felt a little embarrassed.

After they exchanged greetings, Gate pointed toward the two-story mansion beyond the fountain.

"Sir Deweyville is waiting for us."

"All right." Klein brushed a hand over the revolver at his waist.

It was his most reliable means of confronting an enemy.

Because he was in uniform, he could carry the revolver openly in a holster on his hip, making it much easier to draw.

The three men followed the drive around the fountain and approached the front entrance. A servant had already opened the door and stood waiting respectfully beside it.

Pretending to adjust his cap, Klein tapped his glabella twice and activated his Spirit Vision before entering.

Sir Deweyville sat in the hall, massaging his forehead. The square-faced man was clearly exhausted. His blond hair looked dry, and his blue eyes were dull. He seemed to have aged at least five years.

"Good morning, Sir Deweyville." Klein, Tolle, and Gate bowed together.

Sir Deweyville rose and forced a smile.

"Good morning, officers. I hope you can put an end to whatever has been tormenting me."

Klein narrowed his eyes and frowned slightly.

Aside from his exhaustion and low spirits, Spirit Vision revealed nothing abnormal about Sir Deweyville.

That's strange... After a moment's thought, Klein asked, "Sir, in which room did you first hear the moaning?"

"My bedroom." Sir Deweyville shook his head wearily.

"May we examine it?" Klein asked.

"Hasn't it been inspected many times already?" the middle-aged butler interjected.

He clearly failed to recognize Klein as the companion of the kindhearted man who had returned the money Klein supposedly found.

Klein remained composed and smiled.

"Those inspections were performed by my colleagues, not by me."

"Sir, he is the expert assigned by the police department," Tolle said, taking the opportunity to reinforce the introduction.

Deweyville studied the young expert, then said, "Very well. Cullen, take him to my bedroom."

"Sir, I would like you to accompany us," Klein said gravely.

Deweyville hesitated for several seconds.

"If it will help solve the problem..."

He took up his cane and walked feebly toward the stairs. Butler Cullen and several guards surrounded him, ready to offer support if needed.

Klein silently surveyed his surroundings as he followed.

One step, two steps, three steps... They reached the second floor and entered the master bedroom.

Before Klein could examine the room, every hair on his body stood on end.

His spiritual perception was warning him!

Chapter 90: What His Eyes Revealed

Sir Deweyville's bedroom was larger than the living room and dining room of Klein's home combined. It was divided into a sleeping area, a sitting area, a dressing room, a bathroom, and a study furnished with a desk and bookshelves. Every piece of furniture was exquisite, every detail extravagant.

Yet to Klein, the light seemed dimmer inside, and the temperature felt several degrees colder than it had outside.

At the same time, he thought he heard sobs and moans, like the last desperate struggles of the dying.

Klein fell into a momentary trance. Then everything abruptly returned to normal. Bright sunlight poured through the windows and filled the bedroom. The temperature was comfortable, neither hot nor cold. Around him, the police officers, bodyguards, and butler stood in silence.

This... He glanced toward the classically styled but luxurious bed. In its shadows, he sensed pairs of indistinct eyes hovering like moths lingering fearlessly around a gas lamp.

When he took a few steps closer, the images vanished from his Spirit Vision.

It isn't an ordinary wraith or evil spirit... What exactly is it? Klein frowned and searched through the mysticism knowledge he had accumulated.

Judging from what he had seen, the mission would have been simple for a Corpse Collector, Gravedigger, or Spirit Medium. Unfortunately, it did not fall within his own area of expertise.

Suppressing the urge to investigate through divination, Klein slowly surveyed the room, searching for traces that might confirm the possibilities in his mind.

"Inspector," Sir Deweyville began hesitantly. "Have you discovered anything?"

"If it were that easy, I doubt my colleagues would have left the matter unresolved until now," Klein replied, unconsciously glancing at the philanthropist.

Just as he was about to look away, he noticed a faint white human shape reflected behind Sir Deweyville in a mirror.

No, it was composed of many figures overlapping one another, merging into a single distorted white silhouette!

It disappeared in a flash, and Klein seemed to hear faint weeping.

Whew... He exhaled to calm himself. The sight had nearly frightened him into drawing his revolver.

One day, heightened spiritual perception combined with Spirit Vision is going to scare me senseless... Klein relaxed his taut nerves with a private joke, then focused on Sir Deweyville once more.

This time, he noticed something different.

Here in the bedroom, a faint mass of twisted figures flickered around Sir Deweyville, dimming the light in his immediate vicinity.

Every flicker was accompanied by an illusory sob or moan, sounds almost imperceptible to an ordinary person.

Almost imperceptible to ordinary people under normal circumstances... Is that because it's daytime? Klein nodded thoughtfully.

He now had a preliminary explanation for the case.

Resentment was haunting Sir Deweyville: lingering spirituality born from the unresolved emotions of the dead.

If such resentment persisted and gathered strength, it would eventually become a terrifying wrathful spirit.

But Sir Deweyville is a celebrated philanthropist. Even someone as critical as Benson admires him. Why would he be burdened by the resentment of the dead? Is his benevolent reputation merely a facade? Or is this the work of a Beyonder with malicious intentions? Klein considered each possibility with suspicion.

After a moment, he turned to Deweyville.

"Sir, I have several questions."

"Please ask." Deweyville sat down wearily.

Klein organized his thoughts. "Whenever you leave this house for somewhere new, such as the countryside or Backlund, do you enjoy at least half a night's peace before the disturbances return and gradually grow worse? And if you sleep during the day, do you still hear the moans and sobs?"

Deweyville's half-closed eyes suddenly widened, hope shining in their deep-blue depths.

"Yes! Have you found the source of the problem?"

Only then did he realize that prolonged insomnia and mental exhaustion had caused him to omit such an important clue when speaking to the police.

Inspector Tolle relaxed when Klein's question produced a useful answer. He knew the Nighthawk had found a lead.

Sergeant Gate was both surprised and curious. He could not help taking a closer look at Klein, the supposed expert in psychology.

It matches the pattern of gradual entanglement and accumulation... With Deweyville's confirmation, Klein was nearly certain of the cause.

He had two ways to free Sir Deweyville from the burden. He could establish an altar near the man and use ritualistic magic to disperse the resentment entirely. Alternatively, he could use other mysticism methods to locate its source and resolve the underlying problem.

Since Beyonders were expected to keep their powers hidden from ordinary people whenever possible, Klein decided to try the second approach first. He would pray to the Goddess only if that failed.

"Sir, your condition is psychological in nature. It is a problem of the mind," he declared with perfect seriousness, uttering complete nonsense.

Sir Deweyville frowned. "Are you saying I'm mentally ill? That I need to be committed to an asylum?"

"No, it is nothing so severe. In truth, nearly everyone suffers from psychological problems to one degree or another," Klein reassured him smoothly. "Allow me to introduce myself again. I am a psychological expert with the Awwa County Police."

"A psychological expert?" Deweyville and his butler both looked toward Inspector Tolle, whom they knew.

Tolle nodded solemnly, confirming the claim.

"Very well. What must I do for this treatment? And I still don't understand why my butler, bodyguards, and servants can hear the sobbing and moaning as well..." Deweyville clasped the handle of his walking stick with both hands, his expression perplexed.

Klein answered with professional composure. "I will explain once the problem has been resolved.

"Please ask your butler, servants, and bodyguards to leave. Inspector Tolle, Sergeant Gate, you should leave as well. I need a quiet environment for the preliminary treatment."

A "treatment" involving magic... Inspector Tolle added inwardly before nodding to Sir Deweyville.

Deweyville remained silent for more than ten seconds.

"Cullen, take them to the upstairs sitting room."

"Yes, sir." Butler Cullen offered no objection. After all, the request had come from a police officer, a probationary inspector, and an expert in psychology.

Klein watched everyone file out and close the door. Then he addressed the dark-blond, blue-eyed Deweyville.

"Sir, please lie on the bed. Relax and try to sleep."

"All right." Deweyville hung his coat and hat on a rack, walked slowly to the bed, and lay down.

Klein drew all the curtains, plunging the room into darkness.

He removed the topaz pendant from his wrist and quickly used spirit pendulum divination to check for danger. Then he sat in the rocking chair at the foot of the bed, visualized a sphere of light, and entered Cogitation, allowing the spiritual world to unfold before him.

Leaning back in the chair, he sank into a deep sleep and let his Astral Projection make contact with the outside world.

He was using dream divination to place himself within a dreamlike spiritual environment, where he could communicate with each fragment of resentment haunting Sir Deweyville.

Only through communication could he find the answer and solve the problem.

Whoosh! Whoosh! Whoosh!

Mournful sobs echoed in Klein's ears, and he "saw" translucent white figures floating around him.

An agonized groan sounded. Barely retaining his ability to think, Klein reached out and touched one of the figures.

Suddenly, they swarmed toward him like moths rushing into a flame.

The scene before his eyes blurred, and his mind seemed to split in two. One half observed calmly while the other stared into a "mirror."

Within the "mirror," a young woman in work clothes walked through a dust-filled factory. She appeared strong and healthy, but her head throbbed with pain.

Her vision blurred from time to time, and she grew thinner with each passing day.

Someone seemed to call her Charlotte. The same voice said she suffered from hysteria.

Hysteria? She looked into a mirror and saw a faint blue line along her gums.

The view shifted, and Klein saw another young woman named Maty.

She too entered the lead factory young and full of life.

Then one side of her face began to twitch, followed by the arm and leg on the same side of her body.

"You have epilepsy," someone told her as convulsions seized her entire body.

She collapsed, the spasms intensifying until she finally lost consciousness.

Then there was another woman, despondent and dazed as she wandered the streets, her speech increasingly impaired.

She suffered terrible headaches and occasional convulsions, and a blue line marked her gums.

She visited a doctor, who told her, "Lafayette, this is lead poisoning."

The doctor regarded her with pity as she convulsed again. Her body twitched without pause until the last light faded from her eyes.

Image after image unfolded before Klein. Immersed in them, he watched with detached calm.

At last, he understood what had befallen the women.[1]

The workers had been exposed to white lead for extended periods. After years of breathing its dust and handling its powder, they had all died of lead poisoning.

Sir Deweyville owned a lead factory as well as two porcelain factories. All three employed women because their labor was cheaper.

Klein silently watched, but sensed that something remained unexplained.

The resentment left by each death was too weak to affect reality or harm Deweyville, even when gathered together.

Unless... Unless a stronger, more tenacious resentment had drawn all the others into a single whole.

At that moment, he "saw" another young woman.

She could not have been more than eighteen. In the factory, she worked at glazing porcelain.

"Hayley, how have you been feeling? Do you still have headaches? If they become severe, you must tell me. Sir Deweyville has established a rule: anyone with serious headaches must stop working with lead and leave the factory," an older woman said with concern.

Hayley rubbed her forehead and smiled. "It's only a little pain. I'm fine."

"Tell me tomorrow if it gets any worse," the older woman urged.

Hayley agreed. When she returned home, however, she continued massaging her forehead.

Her parents and brother were already there, despair written across their faces.

"Your father and brother lost their jobs..." her mother said, wiping away tears.

Her father and brother hung their heads.

"We'll try to find work at the harbor," they muttered.

"But we won't even have money for bread the day after tomorrow... We may have to move to Lower Street..." Hayley's mother looked at her with reddened eyes. "When are you paid? It's ten soli, isn't it?"

Hayley rubbed her forehead again.

"Saturday. Yes, Saturday."

She said nothing more and remained as quiet as ever. The next day, she returned to the factory and told her supervisor that her headache was gone and she felt perfectly well.

She smiled and walked five kilometers to and from work each day. With every passing day, she massaged her head more frequently.

"You still haven't found another job?" Hayley finally asked her father and brother as she stared at the thin soup simmering beside their black bread.

Her father replied bitterly, "The economy is in a recession. Factories are dismissing workers everywhere, and even the harbor offers only occasional work. I've earned just three soli and seven pence this week."

Hayley sighed and lapsed into her customary silence. She hid her left hand as it began to twitch.

The following morning, she set out for work again. Sunlight bathed the streets, which grew steadily more crowded with pedestrians.

Without warning, convulsions seized her entire body.

She collapsed beside the road, foam spilling from her mouth.

She stared up at the sky as her vision blurred. She saw people walk past and others draw near. She saw a carriage roll by, bearing the Deweyville family crest: a white dove with wings spread as though about to take flight.

She struggled to open her mouth, but no sound emerged.

And so she said nothing, remaining silent as always.

But this time, she was dead.

[1] These women were originally inspired by Jack London's *The People of the Abyss*.

Chapter 91: The Solution

The scene distorted, grew insubstantial, and slowly faded away.

Klein emerged from the dreamlike state, his eyes gradually adjusting to the darkness of the room.

He knew that even with Benson earning one pound and ten soli--thirty soli--a week, supporting their family at the standard of an average household had not been easy.

He had once believed that most workers earned around twenty soli a week.

He remembered Melissa mentioning that on Lower Street, in the Iron Cross Street district, five, seven, or even ten families might be crammed into a single room.

Benson had also told him that events on the Southern Continent had pushed the kingdom's economy into recession over the past few months.

And he knew that a live-in maid, with room and board included, might earn anywhere from three soli and sixpence to six soli a week.

Klein raised a hand and pinched his glabella. He remained silent for a long time, until Sir Deweyville finally spoke.

"Officer, aren't you going to say anything? The psychiatrists I've consulted always talked to me and asked questions in situations like this.

"That said, I feel remarkably peaceful. I almost fell asleep, and I haven't heard any moans or cries yet.

"How did you do it?"

Leaning back in the rocking chair, Klein did not answer. Instead, he asked gently, "Sir, do you know about lead poisoning? Do you understand the dangers of lead?"

Deweyville was silent for several seconds.

"I didn't in the past, but I do now. Are you telling me that my psychological condition comes from guilt--guilt toward the women employed in my lead and porcelain factories?"

Without waiting for Klein's answer, he continued in the manner of a powerful man controlling the course of a negotiation.

"Yes, I did feel guilty before, but I compensated them. The workers in my lead and porcelain factories are paid no less than others in the same trades. In Backlund, lead and porcelain workers receive no more than eight soli a week. I pay ten, sometimes more.

"Heh. Many industrialists accuse me of breaking ranks because my wages make it harder for them to recruit workers. If the repeal of the Grain Act hadn't bankrupted so many farmers and driven them into the cities, those industrialists would have been forced to raise their wages as I did.

"Furthermore, I instructed the factory supervisors to remove anyone suffering recurring headaches or blurred vision from areas where they would be exposed to lead. If their illness is severe, they can even seek assistance from my charitable foundation.

"I believe I've done enough."

Klein's voice remained even and emotionless. "Sir, people of your position sometimes cannot imagine how vital a single week's wages can be to the poor. Losing work for only a week or two may inflict irreversible damage on an entire family and bring them terrible grief."

He paused before continuing. "I'm curious. Why wouldn't a benevolent man like you install equipment in your factories to protect workers from dust and lead poisoning?"

Deweyville stared at the ceiling and laughed bitterly.

"The cost would be more than I could bear. I would no longer be able to compete with the other lead and porcelain companies. I care little about the profits from those parts of my business now. In fact, I would even accept a modest loss. But what would be the point of keeping the factories open if I had to pour money into them indefinitely? It would help only a limited number of workers. It wouldn't establish an industry standard or change conditions in other factories.

"I would merely be paying out of my own pocket to support my employees. I've heard that some factories even use slaves in secret to keep their costs down."

Klein folded his hands and remained silent for a moment.

"Sir, the source of your psychological condition is accumulated guilt, even though you believed that guilt had diminished and disappeared with time. Under normal circumstances, it would have caused no obvious symptoms. But something triggered it, causing every buried problem to erupt at once."

"Something triggered me?" Deweyville asked in confusion, yet with conviction. "I know of no such incident."

The rocking chair swayed gently as Klein explained in a soothing tone, "You fell asleep for several minutes just now, and while you slept, you told me something."

"Hypnosis?" Deweyville guessed, as was his habit.

Klein did not answer directly.

"While riding in your carriage, you once saw a young woman dying on her way to work. Lead poisoning killed her. She had worked in one of your porcelain factories, glazing the wares."

Deweyville rubbed his temples. After a speechless moment, he said uncertainly, "I think something like that did happen... but I can't remember it clearly."

His long struggle with insomnia had left him in such poor condition that he could recall the scene only vaguely.

He tried to remember, then abandoned the effort rather than strain his mind further.

"What was the worker's name?

"More importantly, what must I do to cure this psychological condition?"

Klein answered immediately. "Two things.

"First, the worker who died beside the road was named Hayley Walker. You told me that yourself. Her death was the immediate trigger, so you must find her parents and give them further compensation.

"Second, publicize the dangers of lead through newspapers and magazines. Have your charitable foundation assist more workers who have suffered from exposure. And if you succeed in becoming a member of Parliament, advocate for laws addressing the problem."

Deweyville slowly sat up and laughed self-deprecatingly.

"I will do everything else, but passing such a law... Heh. I believe that would be impossible. We must still compete with foreign nations. A law of that sort could plunge the entire domestic industry into crisis. Factories would fail one after another, and countless workers would lose their jobs. Charitable organizations could never support them all."

He climbed slowly from the bed and straightened his collar. Looking at Klein, he said, "Hayley Walker, correct? I'll have Cullen obtain her records from the porcelain company at once and locate her parents. Officer, please remain here and continue evaluating my mental state."

"All right." Klein rose and smoothed his black-and-white checkered police uniform.

At eleven that morning, Klein sat silently on a sofa in Sir Deweyville's reception room, watching Butler Cullen escort a man and woman inside.

Both had mottled skin and faces already deeply lined with wrinkles. The man stooped slightly, while the woman had a mole beneath one eye.

They closely resembled the parents Klein had seen through Hayley's memories, only older and more haggard. They were nearly skeletal, and their clothing was old and ragged. Klein had even learned that they could no longer afford to remain on Lower Street in the Iron Cross Street district.

Sob...

Klein's spiritual perception detected an icy wind spiraling through the room.

He pinched his glabella and glanced at Sir Deweyville. At some point, a faint, translucent white figure had appeared behind the knight, its shape warped and twisted.

"G-good morning, honorable sir," Hayley's parents stammered with excessive politeness.

Deweyville rubbed his forehead. "Are you Hayley Walker's parents? Doesn't she also have a brother and a two-year-old sister?"

Hayley's mother answered fearfully, "Her--her brother broke his leg at the harbor some time ago. We left him at home to look after his sister."

Deweyville was silent for several seconds before sighing.

"You have my deepest condolences for what happened to Hayley."

The parents' eyes immediately reddened. They began speaking over one another.

"Th-thank you for your kindness.

"The police told us--told us Hayley died of lead poisoning. That's what they called it, isn't it? Oh, my poor child. She was only seventeen. She was always so quiet, but so determined.

"You sent someone to visit us before and paid for her burial. She rests in Raphael Cemetery."

Deweyville glanced at Klein, shifted in his seat, and leaned forward.

"That was an oversight on our part. I owe you an apology," he said gravely.

"I've considered the compensation owed to you--and to Hayley. She earned ten soli a week, correct? That amounts to five hundred and twenty soli, or twenty-six pounds, each year. Let us suppose she could have worked another ten years.

"Cullen, give Hayley's parents three hundred pounds."

"Th-three hundred pounds?" Hayley's parents stared at him in disbelief.

Even at their most prosperous, they had never possessed more than a pound in savings.

They were not the only ones stunned. Shock and envy appeared on the faces of the bodyguards and maids. Even Sergeant Gate drew a sharp breath. He earned only two pounds a week, while the one-chevron constables beneath him received just one.

Amid the silence, Butler Cullen emerged from the study carrying a bulging sack.

He opened it to reveal stacks of currency: some one-pound and five-pound notes, but mostly one- and five-soli bills.

Clearly, Deweyville had ordered his subordinates to obtain smaller denominations from the bank in advance.

"This is an expression of Sir Deweyville's goodwill." After receiving a nod from his employer, Cullen handed the sack to Hayley's parents.

They accepted it, rubbed their eyes, and stared at the money in disbelief.

"No, this--this is far too generous. We cannot accept it," they protested, clutching the sack tightly.

Deweyville's voice was deep and steady. "This is what Hayley deserves."

"Y-you truly are a noble and charitable knight!" Hayley's parents bowed again and again, overcome with emotion.

Irrepressible smiles spread across their faces.

They praised the knight repeatedly, relying on the same few complimentary words they knew. Again and again, they insisted that Hayley would be grateful to him in heaven.

"Cullen, take them home. No--take them to the bank first," Deweyville instructed with a relieved sigh.

Hayley's parents hugged the sack to their chests and hurried toward the door without stopping.

Klein saw the faint translucent figure behind Deweyville reach toward them, hoping to depart in their company. But the smiles on her parents' faces shone with extraordinary brightness, and neither looked back.

The figure faded until it disappeared completely.

At the same instant, the unnatural chill in the reception room vanished.

Throughout the entire encounter, Klein had done nothing but sit in silence, offering no opinion.

"Officer, I feel much better," Deweyville said. "Can you now explain why my butler, servants, and bodyguards heard the cries and moans as well? Surely this wasn't merely my own psychological illness?"

Inspector Tolle, who knew the truth behind the incident, immediately grew tense.

Klein answered without changing his expression.

"In psychology, we call this phenomenon mass hysteria."

Chapter 92: Psychological Expert

"Mass hysteria?" Sir Deweyville, who had consulted numerous psychiatrists over the past month, contemplated the term Klein had used.

Although his butler, bodyguards, and servants were curious, none dared speak without his permission.

Sergeant Gate looked at Klein doubtfully, as though he had never heard of such a concept.

Klein suppressed his habit of tapping his fingertips against the armrest and explained calmly, "Human beings are easily deceived by their senses. Mass hysteria is a psychogenic condition that arises when tension and other factors spread through a group whose members influence one another."

The stream of technical language bewildered Sir Deweyville, Sergeant Gate, and everyone else, making them instinctively inclined to believe him.

"Let me offer a simple example. This comes from a case I previously encountered. A man held a dinner and invited thirty-five guests. Halfway through the meal, he suddenly became nauseated and vomited. He later developed severe diarrhea. After several episodes, he concluded that he was suffering from food poisoning and shared that suspicion with the other guests on the way to the hospital.

"Within two hours, more than thirty of the thirty-five guests had developed diarrhea, while twenty-six reported nausea. Together, they overwhelmed the hospital's emergency room.

"The doctors conducted detailed examinations and cross-checked their findings. They discovered that the first man had never suffered from food poisoning. His stomach had become inflamed because of a change in the weather combined with cold liquor.

"Even more remarkably, none of the other guests who visited the hospital had been poisoned. In fact, not one of them was physically ill.

"That is mass hysteria."

Deweyville nodded slowly in wonder. "I understand. People can deceive themselves surprisingly easily. No wonder Emperor Roselle once said that if a lie is repeated a hundred times, it becomes reality.

"Officer, may I have your name? You are the most capable psychiatrist I've ever met."

"Inspector Moretti." Klein pointed to his epaulet. "Sir, your troubles appear to have been resolved for now. Please try to sleep while I determine whether any problems remain. If you sleep soundly, allow us to leave without waiting for you to awaken."

"Very well." Deweyville rubbed his forehead, took up his cane, and returned upstairs to his bedroom.

Half an hour later, a police carriage rolled away from the fountain outside the Deweyville residence.

After Sergeant Gate disembarked along the way to return to his station, Inspector Tolle turned to Klein and joked, "For a moment, even I believed you were a genuine expert in psychology..."

Before he could finish, he noticed the young man in the black-and-white checkered uniform staring ahead without expression. Klein's eyes were deep and still. Forcing a faint smile, he replied, "I've had some experience with the subject."

Inspector Tolle fell silent and remained so until the carriage stopped outside 36 Zouteland Street.

"Thank you for your assistance. You've finally freed Sir Deweyville from his torment and allowed him to sleep again." Tolle extended his hand and shook Klein's. "Please convey my thanks to Dunn as well."

Klein nodded. "I will."

He climbed the stairs, returned to the Blackthorn Security Company, and knocked before entering the captain's office.

"Finished?" Dunn was waiting for his lunch.

"Finished." Klein massaged his forehead and kept his explanation brief. "The problem originated in Sir Deweyville's lead and porcelain factories. Since they opened, too many workers have died from lead poisoning. Every death left a trace of resentful spirituality attached to him."

"Generally, that wouldn't cause any serious harm. At most, it might produce nightmares." Dunn's extensive experience had brought him across similar cases before.

Klein nodded. "Yes, that would ordinarily be true. Unfortunately, Sir Deweyville once passed a female worker dying from lead poisoning in the street. She collapsed by the roadside and happened to see the Deweyville family crest. She carried powerful feelings of resentment, worry, and longing. Those emotions dissipated only after Sir Deweyville gave three hundred pounds in compensation to her parents, brother, and sister."

"This is a problem of society, one that is hardly rare in the Age of Steam and Machinery." Dunn took out his pipe, inhaled the scent of the tobacco, and sighed. "Linen workers labor in damp conditions and frequently develop bronchitis and diseases of the joints. In factories filled with powder and dust, even substances that aren't poisonous can accumulate in the lungs and cause disease..."

He sighed again. "Enough of this. As the kingdom develops, I believe these problems will eventually be resolved. Klein, shall we find a restaurant tonight and celebrate your becoming an official member?"

Klein considered the offer. "Could we do it tomorrow instead? Captain, I used Spirit Vision for a long time today, then employed dream divination to interact directly with the lingering resentment. I'm particularly exhausted and would like to go home this afternoon to rest. Would that be all right? Afterward, at around four or five, I'll visit the Divination Club and see how its members have reacted to Hanass Vincent's sudden death."

"Of course. That's only sensible." Dunn chuckled. "Tomorrow evening, then. We'll go to Old Will's Restaurant next door. I'll have Rozanne reserve a table."

Klein removed his peaked police cap, stood, and saluted.

"Thank you, Captain. I'll see you tomorrow."

Dunn raised a hand. "Wait. Did you say Sir Deweyville gave the worker's parents three hundred pounds?"

"Yes." Klein immediately understood his concern. "You're worried that possessing so much money might put them in danger?"

Dunn sighed.

"I've seen many similar situations. Give me their address. I'll have Kenley arrange for them to leave Tingen, settle in another city, and begin a new life."

"All right," Klein replied solemnly.

With that settled, he left Dunn's office and entered the break room diagonally opposite. He changed back into his suit and stored the police uniform in his locker.

Klein silently rode a public carriage back to Daffodil Street. After removing his coat and top hat, he reheated the previous night's leftovers and ate them with the last piece of wheat bread.

Then he went upstairs, hung up his clothes, and collapsed onto his bed.

When he awoke, his pocket watch showed ten minutes past two. The sun stood high in the sky, its rays streaming through the clouds.

Bathed in golden light, Klein stood beside his desk and gazed through the oriel window at pedestrians in worn, patched clothing entering and leaving Iron Cross Street.

Whew... He slowly exhaled, finally emerging from his melancholy.

Every journey had to be completed one step at a time. Likewise, he could advance through the Sequences only one level at a time. That was simply how everything worked.

He shook his head, sat down, and began reviewing and organizing everything he had encountered over the past week, reinforcing the important points in his mind so he would not forget them.

Five minutes before three in the afternoon, a towering palace stood above the boundless, silent gray fog. An ancient, mottled bronze table rested within it.

At the place of honor sat a man shrouded in dense gray mist.

Klein leaned against the back of his chair and considered his next move. Then he reached out and touched the crimson stars representing Justice and The Hanged Man.

In Backlund's Empress Borough, Audrey lifted the hem of her dress and hurried toward her bedroom.

Suddenly sensing something, she glanced toward the shadowed balcony. As expected, she found her golden retriever, Susie, sitting there in silence and observing her as always.

Audrey sighed and traced the crimson moon across her chest. She approached and looked down at the dog with an air of authority.

"Susie, that isn't right. You're spying on me. A Spectator should observe openly."

The golden retriever raised her head, looked at her owner, and wagged her tail.

Having admonished her dog, Audrey wasted no more time and continued toward her bedroom.

As she opened and closed the door, a peculiar thought occurred to her.

I wonder whether Mr. Fool would allow Susie into that mysterious space. Then the Tarot Gathering would have four members, and every one of us would be a Beyonder!

No, that wouldn't work. Susie can't speak. If she were asked to share an opinion or explain her thoughts, what would she do? Woof, woof, woof? Howl? Ugh, why am I imitating a dog...

Just imagining it feels absurd. A solemn, mysterious gathering suddenly interrupted by barking... Mr. Fool would surely expel us from the Tarot Gathering immediately...

Audrey locked the door and sat on the edge of her bed. From beneath her pillow, she retrieved an old sheet of yellow-brown paper.

She read it several times, then entered her Spectator state.

Meanwhile, in a certain region of the Sonia Sea, an old sailing ship pursuing the Listener had already departed the Rorsted Archipelago.

Seafarer Alger Wilson worried that the wall clock's mechanism might malfunction, so he entered the captain's cabin half an hour early. He could not risk misjudging the time and allowing his subordinates to see him being pulled into the Tarot Gathering.

A glass of nearly transparent liquor sat before him, its rich aroma rising in delicate wisps.

At the thought of the approaching Gathering, Alger shuddered again. He remembered the limitless fog that had appeared before him in the inn's corridor, and the mysterious Fool seated at its center.

He raised the glass and took a deep swallow, using the burning sensation in his throat to calm his turbulent emotions.

Before long, he regained his usual composure, becoming once again calm and impassive.

Chapter 93: A New Page from the Diary

Above the gray fog, colossal stone pillars supported a majestic divine hall.

Beside the ancient, mottled bronze table, two dark-red masses stretched and resolved into indistinct human figures.

"Good afternoon, Mr. Fool." Her features blurred by the surrounding haze, Audrey rose, bowed, and smiled. "It's a pity we have no wine here. Otherwise, we could raise a toast to the success of your experiment."

She was referring to the ritualistic magic.

"You are even more powerful than we imagined," Alger Wilson added in praise.

As always, Klein sat enveloped in thick fog. He lowered his right hand and replied in his usual tone, as though the outcome were only natural.

"Good. This proves we're on the right path. If some matter prevents you from attending a Monday gathering, perform the ritual and inform me. Simply replace the line 'I pray for a good dream' with your reason."

"All right," Audrey agreed at once. "Mr. Fool, I obtained another page from Emperor Roselle's diary. I believe I still owe you one more."

"I spent the entire week at sea and found no new pages." Alger placed his right hand against his chest and bowed apologetically.

"It doesn't matter. I expected this commission to take a long time." Klein leaned back in his chair and tapped the armrest with his index finger. Looking toward Miss Justice, he said, "You may reproduce the diary page now."

Audrey bowed slightly. "As you wish."

A fountain pen appeared before her. Recalling the symbols she had memorized, she carefully transcribed them.

Within seconds, the sheet of goatskin parchment was filled with neatly arranged, peculiar symbols.

After checking her work, she set down the pen. "It's finished."

Klein raised a hand, and the parchment appeared in his palm.

He lowered his gaze and began reading without betraying any emotion.

"July 9th. An interesting question suddenly occurred to me. If the Sequence pathways are also called the 'Blessings of the Divinities' or the 'Paths of the Divine,' why is the stone tablet recording all twenty-two complete pathways called the Blasphemy Slate? Blasphemy--what an intriguing word... Who, exactly, was blasphemed?

"And who created the Blasphemy Slate? How could anyone possess knowledge of every Sequence pathway? What other information was recorded on it? I truly want to see it...

"July 12th. Today, I realized something else. Sealed Artifacts are a crucial part of a Church's overall strength, even though some of them are extraordinarily dangerous. Among the seven Churches, the Church of the God of Craftsmanship possesses the fewest Sealed Artifacts, and those it does possess are relatively less dangerous... Did I join an organization with no future? No, I should look at it differently. Only a blank sheet of paper can receive a great painting. A weaker organization is the perfect place for me to demonstrate my abilities!

"July 14th. I met the mysterious Mr. Zaratul again. I never expected him to be the leader of an ancient organization--the Secret Order!"

Klein's pupils contracted when he reached that sentence, and he nearly let his surprise show.

The Church of Evernight's records had said only that the Zaratul family had certain ties to the Secret Order. Emperor Roselle's diary now confirmed that the mysterious Mr. Zaratul had actually been its leader.

It seems indisputable that the Secret Order controls the Seer pathway...

While Klein read, Audrey glanced toward him and instinctively attempted to observe his reaction.

However, the dense fog completely concealed him from her sight.

After a brief moment of confusion, Audrey realized what she had done and hurriedly turned toward the other illusory crimson figure.

I was too reckless, too presumptuous, too foolish! I actually tried to observe Mr. Fool... Fortunately, he isn't angry. Audrey secretly stuck out her tongue and pretended to admire the surroundings. She was only a step away from humming a cheerful tune.

Alger sat quietly, never lifting his gaze from the long bronze table. He knew his place and behaved as though he truly stood in the presence of a god.

Klein collected himself and continued reading the remainder of the diary.

"After learning that I had become a Savant, Mr. Zaratul remarked that I had chosen a difficult but comparatively safe path. I asked why, but he merely smiled and told me the Sequence pathways conceal secrets beyond my imagination. I couldn't resist asking which pathway he had chosen. He told me his Sequence 9 was Seer.

"I deliberately mocked him, asking whether every Seer spoke only in half-truths and refused to explain anything clearly. Besides, he was obviously a powerful High-Sequence Beyonder. Surely he no longer needed to behave like a Seer!

"Mr. Zaratul told me it was a habit left over from his days as a Seer, and that it was also an effective way to arouse my curiosity and persuade me to cooperate. He wanted my help stealing a dangerous Sealed Artifact from the Church of the God of Craftsmanship--a relic of the Antigonus family.

"Obviously, this must wait until I become a core member of the Church. I asked Mr. Zaratul how long it would take to digest a potion through the acting method and how I could determine when digestion was complete.

"He told me that in the lower Sequences, strict adherence to the acting method would allow the potion to be digested within half a year. In the fastest cases, it might take only a month. As for determining whether digestion was complete, the standard was simple: every Beyonder would immediately sense it when the time came. It is what it is.

"I pressed him for details, but he only smiled at me.

"To hell with his smiles! Once I become a High-Sequence Beyonder, I'll beat up every Seer I meet!"

...Rest in peace, Emperor. Klein reread the page several times before looking toward Justice and The Hanged Man.

"My apologies for keeping you waiting."

"It is our honor," Audrey replied, still so astonished that she forgot to maintain her Spectator state.

She looked toward The Hanged Man and carefully organized her words.

"Where can I find the Psychology Alchemists?"

The Psychology Alchemists... Klein suddenly remembered the man who had purchased supplementary ingredients for the Spectator potion at Tingen's underground market.

Could he be a member of the Psychology Alchemists?

As Klein considered how he might approach the man, The Hanged Man shook his head.

"Miss Justice, first, I have no useful information. Second, I don't believe you should hurry to seek out the Psychology Alchemists. Your present priority should be completely digesting the Spectator potion."

Audrey glanced toward The Fool, but he showed no intention of joining the discussion. She nodded in disappointment.

"I only wanted enough time to prepare so I could approach them naturally. Very well. Then how will I know when I've digested the Spectator potion and can stop acting? Is there a clear standard? I'm already close to the point where I no longer feel irritable or hear constant murmuring."

Alger looked toward The Fool in the fog, but again saw no sign that he intended to speak. After some thought, Alger answered, "Without the acting method, the usual rule is to wait three years and confirm that you no longer suffer agitation, auditory hallucinations, or visions. There is also a simple test. Exhaust your body completely. If you still hear no crazed whispers and see nothing unnatural, you should be ready to advance.

"As for the acting method, I've only recently learned of it myself. It appears effective, so I doubt you'll need three years."

That wasn't helpful at all... Three years is far too long... Audrey complained inwardly.

Just then, she heard a finger tapping an armrest.

Audrey froze, then turned with delight. The Fool was tapping the edge of the long table.

Alger straightened in his chair and waited attentively.

Klein spoke in his customary tone. "For Low-Sequence Beyonders, strict adherence to the acting method should allow complete digestion within half a year. It may even take as little as a month."

He looked toward Justice and added, "As for the sign that digestion is complete, you will know when it happens. It requires no instruction."

"One month... Wonderful! Thank you, Mr. Fool!" Audrey exclaimed, overflowing with joy.

Miss Justice, don't assume you're the chosen one. The important part was 'within half a year'... Klein raised his right hand and rested it near his lips.

"Half a year..." Alger repeated softly.

Audrey detected joy, relief, and profound doubt in his voice.

What is he suspicious of? As she wondered, she asked, "Mr. Fool, have you considered admitting more members?"

Klein leaned back casually, having prepared an answer long ago.

"This began as an experiment, so I gave little thought to expanding the gatherings.

"Now that they have become regular meetings, however, new members must be chosen with care. Secrecy is our guiding principle."

Audrey nodded thoughtfully. "In other words, we need a process of observation, recommendation, and testing. Yes, a proper process."

"You may understand it that way," Klein confirmed.

Meanwhile, he was considering how to ask about the Secret Order and the Clown potion.

How can I phrase the questions in a way that suits my supposed status? Klein found himself in a difficult position.

When Justice appeared to have nothing more to say, Alger took the initiative.

"I've heard that a Listener from the Aurora Order is searching for traces of the True Creator--the sacred abode proclaimed by their faith."

"The True Creator?" Audrey asked in confusion.

"An ancient entity worshiped by numerous secret organizations and cults. They believe the Creator did not perish completely and that the core He left behind became the True Creator," Alger explained briefly. "Since the beginning of the Fifth Epoch, the True Creator has appeared in many forms, including the Hanged Giant and the Eye Behind the Shadow Curtains. Heh, many believe Emperor Roselle drew upon images of the True Creator when designing the tarot deck. That is why it contains The Hanged Man."

At that point, he turned toward Klein.

"Mr. Fool, there was nothing incorrect in what I said, was there?"

Is he testing my opinion of the True Creator? Klein recalled the blood-covered man on the cross that Captain Dunn had seen in Hanass Vincent's dream, and an answer immediately occurred to him.

Don't both hanging and shadows carry sinister implications?

He chuckled softly.

"I prefer to call Him the Fallen Creator."

Chapter 94: The Hidden Sage

"The Fallen Creator... Fallen..." Alger contemplated The Fool's words and sank into thought.

What struck him most was The Fool's relaxed, natural, almost indifferent manner.

He spoke as though he and the True Creator were equals!

Had Alger not witnessed the previous ritual, he might have assumed The Fool was merely bluffing, inflating his status to intimidate Justice and him. Now, however, Alger believed that even if The Fool stood below the True Creator, He could not be far beneath that level.

It's dangerous, but it's also an opportunity... Alger murmured inwardly. Then he smiled and said, "Mr. Fool, your description is indeed more appropriate. According to our observations, Beyonders who believe in the True Creator--no, the Fallen Creator--are considerably more likely to lose control. Most of the rest become psychopaths."

That agrees with the Nighthawks' intelligence... And those so-called psychopaths haven't necessarily lost their minds. Rather, their beliefs and values have become distorted... Klein maintained his posture but did not continue the subject.

He was still considering how to inquire about the Secret Order and the Clown potion, but could find no way to pose the questions without compromising his persona.

What a pity this Gathering is nothing like an online forum. Otherwise, I could create a second account to ask all the questions that would be awkward for The Fool... Perhaps someday I'll learn mirror-related magic and be able to try it. I could even make half the members here my alternate identities...

There are twenty-two chairs and twenty-two cards in a tarot deck. The numbers match perfectly. Yet when I "created" this divine hall, I hadn't even chosen the name The Fool, much less conceived of a Tarot Club. Hmm... Could the chairs symbolize the twenty-two Sequence pathways?

I wished for a divine hall, and one appeared. If I wished for another identity, would one appear as well...?

Seeing The Fool remain silent within the dense gray fog, Audrey asked with both apprehension and curiosity, "That sounds terrifying. Mr. Hanged Man, could you give us a detailed account of the mysterious organizations and secret cults? I rarely have any opportunity to encounter them in my daily life. I can learn about them only through the two of you. I'm willing to pay. What would you like in exchange?"

Excellent question! Miss Justice, you're practically acting as my alternate account... The Hanged Man will surely mention the Secret Order. You're the best! Klein's thoughts quickened, but neither his expression nor his posture betrayed his excitement.

Alger considered for a moment. "I need money--one thousand pounds. Preferably not banknotes bearing serial numbers. Newly mined gemstones would also suffice, valued according to the average monthly price listed by the Backlund Jewelry Exchange."

One thousand pounds? That's an enormous sum. It could buy a house on an affluent street in Tingen! Very few people could produce that much at once... The captain might earn roughly that in a year, perhaps? Hayley's death compensation was only three hundred pounds... Although Miss Justice is a noblewoman, she clearly hasn't inherited her family's fortune. She probably receives only an annual allowance... No wonder The Hanged Man offered to accept gemstones. Klein had become acutely sensitive to sums of money. Fortunately, the fog concealed him.

An unmarried woman or widow could live respectably on two thousand pounds!

Properly invested, that amount could reliably yield around a hundred pounds a year.

"One thousand pounds?" Audrey asked in surprise. Then, to Klein's astonishment, she answered cheerfully, "No problem. Should I send it to the same address as before?"

From Miss Justice's tone, she considers that cheap? Klein deliberately refrained from looking at her.

Alger remained silent for a full twenty seconds.

"Yes. Send it to the Warrior & Sea Bar on Pelican Street in Pritz Harbor's White Rose Borough. Tell the proprietor, Williams, that it is what the 'Captain' requested."

"All right." Audrey leaned back and assumed the detached posture of a Spectator. "Mr. Hanged Man, you may begin."

Alger looked toward The Fool. After a moment's deliberation, he spoke slowly.

"Let us begin with the Moses Ascetic Order, the oldest secret organization. Of course, many believe the earliest secret organizations were actually the Church of Evernight, the Church of Earth Mother, and the Church of the God of Combat."

"The people who say that must belong to the Church of Storms, the Church of the Eternal Blazing Sun, or the Church of the God of Knowledge and Wisdom," Audrey retorted unhappily.

The Church of the Goddess was once a secret organization? This was the first time Klein had heard such a claim.

What exactly happened during the Fourth Epoch--or the Third?

Alger smiled. "The truth lies buried in ancient history. Only one fact is certain: no one has ever claimed that the Church of Storms, the Church of the Eternal Blazing Sun, or the Church of the God of Knowledge and Wisdom began as secret organizations.

"Let us save time and return to the main subject. The Moses Ascetic Order was founded by several humans who had read the Blasphemy Slate. They believed in a non-anthropomorphic god known as the Hidden Sage.

"Although described as a god, the Hidden Sage was closer to an idea or a law of nature. For instance, the belief that everything can be expressed through numbers led to the Hidden Sage being regarded as the embodiment of spiritual numerology. Likewise, if knowledge was supreme, then the Hidden Sage was knowledge itself. Thus, the early Moses Ascetic Order was a highly respectable organization and maintained good relations with the major Churches.

"Its members practiced asceticism to resist losing control and to counteract the potion's lingering influence. They guarded the Order's secrets rigorously and observed strict moral and religious precepts. They also believed that humans continually reincarnated after death...

"The Sequence 9 potion they possessed was called Mystery Pryer. The term 'warlock' was also popularized by their organization."

Audrey listened closely and immediately caught an implication in his wording.

"You said the Moses Ascetic Order used to be respectable. Is that no longer the case?"

Alger gave an almost imperceptible nod.

"Correct. They have fallen into corruption and are now an evil organization."

"Why? Their beliefs sound perfectly reasonable," Audrey said in confusion.

Klein wondered the same thing. The records available at his security clearance did not explain why the Moses Ascetic Order had fallen from grace.

Alger glanced at the unfathomable Fool before answering tersely.

"I don't know the true reason. History may have buried it. However, I have heard one terrifying explanation.

"According to that account, the Moses Ascetic Order descended into corruption because the god they worshiped--the Hidden Sage--came to life!

"He became the personification of an evil god!"

"Came to life? But... how?" Audrey asked incredulously, unable to imagine such a thing.

Without realizing it, she had abandoned her Spectator state.

It's like a ghost story, except the ghost is a god... A surge of emotion stirred within Klein as well.

"I'm afraid no one knows the answer." Alger had nearly added, Perhaps Mr. Fool does, but suppressed the impulse.

He had already ventured close to danger once.

Alger clearly remembered a verse from *The Book of Storms*, 5:7: "Thou shalt not test God!"

Audrey composed herself and did not press him further. She gestured for him to continue.

Klein maintained his posture and silence, comparing The Hanged Man's account with what he already knew.

By the end, four points stood out to him.

First, during the Fourth Epoch, the Demoness Sect had also been known as the Demoness Family. Its membership had been small, and its beliefs passed down through bloodlines. They killed the fathers of their children and abandoned all male infants, leaving the organization entirely female. At least, that was Alger's account; Klein had no present means of verifying it.

Second, the Numinous Episcopate, which worshiped Death, and the Rose School of Thought, which favored bloody sacrificial rites, had both originated on the Southern Continent. Following the colonial era, attacks by the seven Churches had nearly eradicated them there. Consequently, they had begun spreading into the Northern Continent.

Third, the modern Psychology Alchemists resembled the early Moses Ascetic Order. They believed in a non-anthropomorphic existence and held that the human mind could transform everything.

Fourth, the Secret Order was less active than any other hidden organization and therefore the least understood. Whenever its members appeared, they seemed to be pursuing or searching for something.

What are they pursuing? What are they searching for? Klein suddenly recalled the diary page he had just read. Zaratul, leader of the Secret Order, had cooperated with Roselle because he wanted a relic left behind by the Antigonus family.

And this time, they appeared while searching for the lost Antigonus family notebook... Klein narrowed his eyes slightly, sensing that he had uncovered the key to the Secret Order's behavior.

They're searching for everything the Antigonus family left behind!

Klein suppressed the impulse to tap the table as thought after thought arose.

So they seek remnants of the Antigonus family?

Then should I concentrate on those remnants if I want to obtain the Clown potion formula from the Secret Order?

After a further exchange of information, Klein announced the Gathering's conclusion.

"As you wish." Audrey and Alger rose together.

When Klein severed the connections, their figures shattered and vanished. He rubbed his glabella, then tried using his thoughts to conjure an alternate identity.

A figure appeared at the far end of the long bronze table. It wore a black tuxedo and silk top hat, but its expression was vacant and its movements stiff and awkward. Even shrouded by gray fog, it was plainly unnatural.

That won't work... After several more experiments, Klein sighed and abandoned the idea.

He tried a few other things while remaining in the place of honor above the gray fog. Recalling Audrey's suggestion, he curiously turned his attention to the illusory crimson stars.

After a moment of silence, Klein began to pray, attempting to provide a response without establishing a direct connection to any of them.

In the ensuing stillness, none of the dozen or so nearby crimson stars responded.

Must I first pull someone above the gray fog before I can answer their prayer? Klein nodded thoughtfully, somewhat disappointed.

He did not wish to violate another person's will by dragging them forcibly into this mysterious space.

Hmm... Klein was preparing to leave when, out of habit, he touched a nearby illusory crimson star.

At that instant, he sensed a faint, almost imperceptible prayer coming from deep within it!

Chapter 95: The Supplicant

"A prayer?"

Klein's thoughts stirred. Using the same method with which he had once spied on The Hanged Man, he extended his spirituality toward the crimson star.

A hazy, distorted image appeared before him. He could faintly make out a blond youth kneeling before a flawless crystal ball.

The young man wore close-fitting black clothes in a style quite different from contemporary Loen fashion. They more closely resembled the traditional dress of the Feysac Empire and Intis Republic that Klein had seen in magazines.

The youth occupied a dark room filled with old furniture. Light flashed through it from time to time, but Klein could hear neither thunder nor the patter of rain.

Within the image, the young man pressed his clasped hands to his forehead, fingers interlaced. He bowed forward and prayed without pause, his heavy accent buzzing in Klein's ears.

Klein listened intently, only to discover an awkward problem.

He could not understand a word.

It was a language he had never encountered in his life!

...To think that I, the mysterious ruler of this world above the gray fog, can't even understand a foreign language... Klein laughed at himself. Indignant, he listened again with greater concentration than he had ever devoted to an English comprehension exam back on Earth.

As the prayer repeated, he gradually noticed something.

Although he had never studied the language, it bore similarities to Ancient Feysac.

Father... Mother... Those are probably the meanings of those two words, aren't they? The language resembles Ancient Feysac, but there are clear differences... Klein frowned in thought. Ancient Feysac was widely used during the Fourth Epoch and served as the root of every modern language in this era. And languages continue to evolve... I can't be certain yet.

He listened several more times and eliminated modern languages such as Loenese, Feysacian, and Intisian.

Could it be a dialect of Ancient Feysac, like the language used in the Antigonus family's notebook? Klein tapped the edge of the bronze table and gave an almost imperceptible nod. There is another possibility. Ancient Feysac didn't emerge from nowhere. It evolved from Jotun, the language of the Giants... The Feysac Empire in the north has always claimed that its people carry Giant blood. Perhaps this is ancient Jotun.

Lacking sufficient knowledge to proceed, Klein stopped. He withdrew his spirituality, and the image and voice vanished.

He had no intention of immediately pulling the praying youth above the gray fog. First, he wanted to understand what the young man was saying.

Before that, of course, he would need to observe the supplicant repeatedly and conduct a few basic tests.

Whew. Klein exhaled and leaned back in his chair.

He wrapped himself in spirituality and simulated the sensation of falling.

After "revising" Roselle's diary, Klein changed into formal clothes and set out for the Divination Club.

Despite his increased salary, he still took public transportation. He did, however, indulge himself by supporting Mrs. Wendy's business, spending one and a half pence on a cup of sweet iced tea to ward off the afternoon heat.

Upon reaching Howes Street, Klein tossed the empty cup into a trash can and climbed to the second floor.

Before entering, he pinched his glabella and activated Spirit Vision.

The moment he stepped into the reception hall, he sensed a faint, lingering sorrow.

The pretty receptionist Angelica sat at her desk, her slightly reddened eyes unfocused.

"Grief fades with time," Klein said in a gentle but steady voice as he approached.

Angelica looked up sharply and murmured in confusion, "Mr. Moretti..."

She quickly collected herself. "Y-you already know about Mr. Vincent?

"Of course. I forgot that you're an exceptional fortune-teller."

Klein sighed at the appropriate moment.

"My divination showed me only the broadest outline of what happened... What exactly befell Mr. Vincent?"

"The proprietor told us Mr. Vincent suffered a heart attack in his sleep and departed this world peacefully." Tears filled Angelica's eyes as she spoke. "He was so friendly and courteous, a true gentleman. He served as a spiritual mentor to so many members. H-he was still so young..."

"I'm sorry for raising such a painful subject." Klein offered no further consolation and walked slowly toward the meeting room.

Angelica wiped her eyes and nose with a handkerchief. Then she called after him, "Mr. Moretti, what would you like to drink?"

"Black tea."

Klein preferred black tea to coffee, although he found the club's tea rather ordinary.

In truth, he would have preferred ginger beer or sweet iced tea. But in a formal setting, a gentleman could hardly behave like a child...

As it was Monday, only five or six members occupied the meeting room. Through Spirit Vision, Klein saw a different emotional color around each of them. Some were grieving, some appeared listless, while others were relatively unaffected.

Their reactions are all quite normal... Klein nodded slightly, carrying his cane as he found a seat.

He was about to deactivate Spirit Vision when Angelica entered and approached him.

"Mr. Moretti, a client is asking for you. Well, it's the same gentleman as last time," the beautiful receptionist said quietly.

"You still remember him?" Klein asked with a smile.

Hmm. I wonder whether he purchased the miraculous medicine as I advised... Does he still require surgery?

Angelica covered her mouth. "He was the only person willing to spend an entire afternoon at the club waiting for a divination."

Without another word, Klein took up his cane, rose, and walked outside.

In the reception hall, he found the man who had sought his services days earlier. The aura around Bogda's liver had returned to its normal color, and his general health had improved considerably.

"Congratulations. Good health is a wonderful thing." Klein smiled and offered his hand.

Bogda stared for a moment, then eagerly seized Klein's right hand in both of his.

"Mr. Moretti, you truly can 'see' my condition!

"Yes, I've completely recovered! The doctors questioned me over and over and performed one examination after another, but they simply couldn't believe that I had recovered so suddenly!"

As Bogda ecstatically described his recovery, Klein calmly confirmed one fact: the apothecary at Lawson's Folk Herb Store was undoubtedly a Beyonder.

Klein had seen the severity of Bogda's liver disease. Neither ordinary herbs nor contemporary medicine could have cured him completely within a few days. Only Beyonder powers offered a plausible explanation.

Combined with what had happened to Glacis, there could be no other answer.

"I must repent before God. I actually doubted you and that miraculous physician." Still gripping Klein's hand, Bogda poured out his shame and gratitude. "...Those ten pounds were worth every penny. They bought back my life!"

What? Ten pounds? You paid ten pounds for the miraculous medicine, but only eight pence for my divination... Eight pence... eight pence... pence... Klein stared in a daze.

At last, Bogda released him and stepped back, beaming. He bowed with genuine reverence.

"I've come today to express my gratitude. Thank you, Master Moretti. You showed me the way and saved my life."

"This was merely the outcome purchased by your divination fee. You needn't thank anyone." Klein raised his head slightly and gazed at the line where the wall met the ceiling, every inch of him exuding the aura of a charlatan.

"You are a true seer," Bogda praised. "Next, I'm going to Vlad Street to thank the apothecary and purchase the other medicine he recommended."

"Haven't you already recovered?" Klein expertly concealed the surprise in his voice.

Bogda glanced around. After confirming that the receptionist was not listening, he gave a quiet chuckle.

"The doctor mentioned an herbal preparation containing mummy powder. He said it was a remedy that would satisfy both men and women... I didn't believe him then, but I have no doubts now."
